| semantic_coherence_of_visual_description | Evaluates whether the answer’s visual description maintains internal logical consistency — including |
|                                          | consistent object states, spatial relationships, and attribute associations — without contradicting  |
|                                          | itself or introducing semantically incompatible claims. Applicable only when the answer contains     |
|                                          | multiple visual elements or attributes that must coexist logically (e.g., ‘the red car is parked     |
|                                          | behind the tree and has a blue stripe’ — if blue stripe contradicts red color, it’s incoherent).     |
|                                          | This criterion is inapplicable to single-object descriptions or questions not requiring multi-       |
|                                          | element integration. It does not penalize stylistic vagueness or contextual interpretation — only    |
|                                          | logical contradictions within the visual description. Workers must verify whether the answer’s       |
|                                          | claims are semantically compatible — even if visually plausible — and penalize contradictions that   |
|                                          | arise from misaligned or incompatible visual attributes. This criterion isa tiebreaker only when    |
|                                          | both answers are factually correct and visually grounded — and only for multi-element descriptions   |
|                                          | with internal logical tension. It should not be applied to aesthetic, interpretive, or inferential   |
|                                          | descriptions — only to descriptions that require consistent integration ofvisual components.        |
|    appropriateness_of_verbosity_level    | Assesses whether the answer’s level of detail is proportionate to the question’s intent — avoiding   |
|                                          | excessive verbosity for simple queries and omitting critical details for complex ones. Applicable    |
|                                          | only to questions that explicitly demand a certain level of detail (e.g., ‘briefly describe’ vs.     |
|                                          | ‘detailed breakdown’). This criterion is inapplicable to binary, yes/no, or interpretive questions — |
|                                          | which are evaluated under other criteria. Workers must verify whether the verbosity level matches    |
|                                          | the question’s scope — penalizing overly verbose answers that include non-essential details — or     |
|                                          | overly sparse answers that omit semantically required elements. This criterion is a tiebreaker only  |
|                                          | when both answers are factually correct and visually grounded — and only for questions where         |
|                                          | verbosity is explicitly contextualized (e.g., ‘summarize’ vs. ‘expand’). It should not be applied to |
|                                          | aesthetic, stylistic, or inferential descriptions — only to descriptive orinventory-style questions |
|                                          | where detail level is explicitly modulated by the question.                          |
|           clarity_of_language            | Evaluates whether the answer’s language is unambiguous, syntactically correct, and free from         |
|                                          | confusing phrasing — ensuring that visual claims are communicated without semantic obfuscation or    |
|                                          | structural ambiguity. Applicable only when the answer’s wording could reasonably be misinterpreted — |
|                                          | even if visually correct — due to unclear syntax, passive voice, or ambiguous pronouns. This         |
|                                          | criterion is inapplicable to poetic, metaphorical, or stylistically dense language — which is        |
|                                          | allowed if visually grounded — and only to questions requiring literal visual description. Workers   |
|                                          | must verify whether the language clearly conveys visual intent — penalizing phrases that could be    |
|                                          | misread (e.g., ‘the object is near the tree’ vs. ‘the object is nearby thetree’ — if the latter     |
|                                          | implies proximity to multiple trees, it’s ambiguous). This criterion is a tiebreaker only when both  |
|                                          | answers are factually correct and visually grounded — and only for answerswith linguistically       |
|                                          | ambiguous phrasing. It should not be applied to aesthetic, interpretive, or inferential descriptions |
|                                          | — only to literal visual descriptions where clarity is essential for accurate visual communication.  |

+------------------------------------------------+------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
| calibrated_uncertainty_in_contextual_questions | This criterion evaluates whether the answer appropriately expresses uncertainty when the visual      |
|                                                | evidence is ambiguous, insufficient, or occluded — especially in contextual, inferential, or         |
|                                                | interpretive questions — without over- or under-stating confidence. It is applicable only when the   |
|                                                | question is not literal or inventory-style (e.g., ‘what is the mood?’, ‘how does this scene feel?’,  |
|                                                | ‘what might be happening?’) and visual evidence is ambiguous or incomplete. The answer must use      |
|                                                | calibrated uncertainty markers (e.g., ‘likely’, ‘appears to be’, ‘based on visual evidence’,         |
|                                                | ‘possibly’) that match the degree of visual ambiguity — not overconfident claims for low-certainty   |
|                                                | situations, nor overly cautious phrasing for high-certainty cases. This criterion is inapplicable to |
|                                                | binary, factual, or literal questions where visual evidence is unambiguous. It is a tiebreaker only  |
|                                                | when both answers are factually consistent and visually grounded — and only for interpretive or      |
|                                                | inferential contexts where uncertainty is measurable. Workers must verify that the uncertainty level |
|                                                | is proportionate to the visual ambiguity — and penalize answers thatare either overconfident (e.g., |
|                                                | claiming certainty where evidence is weak) or overly cautious (e.g.,hedging when visual evidence is |
|                                                | clear). This criterion should not be applied to aesthetic, compositional, or stylistic descriptions  |
|                                                | — only to questions where uncertainty can be meaningfully calibratedbased on visual evidence.       |
|           robustness_to_visual_noise           | This criterion evaluates whether the answer’s visual claims remain valid or are appropriately        |
|                                                | qualified under conditions of visual noise — such as low resolution,compression artifacts, motion   |
|                                                | blur, or occlusion — without introducing hallucinations or misrepresentations. It is applicable only |
|                                                | when the question demands visual claims that must withstand visual degradation — e.g., identifying   |
|                                                | an object in a blurry image, recognizing text under noise, or detecting color in low-contrast areas. |
|                                                | The answer must either correctly identify the visual element despitenoise — or qualify its claim    |
|                                                | with uncertainty markers — without inventing details not supported by the noisy visual context. This |
|                                                | criterion is inapplicable to static, high-resolution, or clearly visible visual elements — and only  |
|                                                | applies to questions where visual noise is a factor or explicitly simulated. It is a tiebreaker only |
|                                                | when both answers are factually correct and visually grounded — and only for questions involving     |
|                                                | visual noise. Workers must verify that the answer’s claims are either robust to noise or             |
|                                                | appropriately hedged — and penalize hallucinations or misrepresentations that arise from noise. This |
|                                                | criterion should not be applied to aesthetic, interpretive, or inferential descriptions — only to    |
|                                                | literal visual claims where noise is a measurable variable.                                |
|      logical_coherence_of_visual_sequence      | This criterion evaluates whether the answer’s visual claims follow acoherent, internally consistent |
|                                                | sequence — particularly for multi-step or sequential visual reasoning (e.g., ‘what happened next?’,  |
|                                                | ‘what is the progression?’, ‘how does this unfold?’) — without introducing logical gaps,             |
|                                                | contradictions, or unsupported causal chains. It is applicable only when the question explicitly     |
|                                                | involves a sequence of visual events, actions, or transformations — and the answer includes a multi- |
|                                                | step narrative or logical progression. The answer must maintain logical flow — with each visual      |
|                                                | claim supporting or being logically derived from the previous — and must not introduce unsupported   |
|                                                | transitions, non-sequiturs, or misattributed actions. This criterionis inapplicable to static       |
|                                                | images, single-event questions, or questions without sequential context. It is a tiebreaker only     |
|                                                | when both answers are factually correct and visually grounded — and only for questions involving     |
|                                                | visual sequences. Workers must verify that the visual sequence is internally consistent — and        |
|                                                | penalize answers that break logical flow or introduce unsupported transitions. This criterion should |
|                                                | not be applied to aesthetic, stylistic, or inferential descriptions — only to questions requiring    |
|                                                | logical progression of visual events.                                |

calibrated_uncertainty_in_contextual_questions
+-----------------------------------------------------+------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
|                      Criterion                      | Description                                     |
+-----------------------------------------------------+------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
| appropriateness_of_visual_element_abstraction_level | This criterion evaluates whether the answer’s description of visual elements matches the appropriate |
|                                                     | level of abstraction for the question’s intent — avoiding overly granular details when the question  |
|                                                     | is high-level or overly generalized when the question demands precision. It is applicable only to    |
|                                                     | questions that explicitly demand a specific level of detail (e.g., ‘describe the scene broadly’ vs.  |
|                                                     | ‘list all visible objects and their attributes’). The answer must not over-abstract (e.g., reducing  |
|                                                     | a detailed object to a vague category when the question requires specificity) or under-abstract      |
|                                                     | (e.g., listing every pixel or texture when the question asks for a high-level summary). It is a      |
|                                                     | tiebreaker only when both answers are factually correct and visually grounded — and only for         |
|                                                     | questions where the abstraction level is explicitly relevant. Workers must verify that the           |
|                                                     | abstraction level aligns with the question’s literal demand — and penalize mismatched levels (e.g.,  |
|                                                     | describing a car’s paint color in a question asking for ‘overall scene mood’). This criterion should |
|                                                     | not be applied to literal, inventory-style, or compositional questions — only to questions that      |
|                                                     | explicitly demand abstraction level control. It is not a substitute for visual grounding or factual  |
|                                                     | consistency — and must be evaluated only after those are confirmed. The criterion must be applied    |
|                                                     | conservatively — and must not override or substitute for other criteria. Workers must not penalize   |
|                                                     | answers for being overly verbose or concise — only for mismatched abstraction levels. This criterion |
|                                                     | is not applicable to aesthetic, interpretive, or inferential questions — and must be explicitly      |
|                                                     | excluded from evaluation for such contexts. The description must include a clear example: for a      |
|                                                     | question asking ‘what is the object?’, an answer describing ‘a red sedan with alloy wheels and a     |
|                                                     | sunroof’ is appropriate — while ‘a car’ is under-abstracted if the question demands precision. For a |
|                                                     | question asking ‘what is the scene like?’, ‘a bustling city street’ is appropriate — while ‘a red    |
|                                                     | sedan with alloy wheels and a sunroof parked near a traffic light’ is over-abstracted. The criterion |
|                                                     | must be applied only when the question’s intent explicitly demands a specific level of abstraction — |
|                                                     | and must not be used to penalize factually correct answers for being ‘too detailed’ or ‘too vague’.  |
|                                                     | It must also explicitly state that it does not apply to questions requiring literal, measurable, or  |
|                                                     | compositional answers — and must defer to other criteria (e.g.,specificity, completeness) for such  |
|                                                     | cases. Finally, it must clarify that ‘appropriate abstraction level’ is determined by the question’s |
|                                                     | literal demand — not by the worker’s subjective judgment — and must be evaluated only after visual   |
|                                                     | grounding and factual consistency are verified.                                     |

appropriateness_of_visual_element_abstraction_level

|                   Criterion                    | Description                                |
+------------------------------------------------+------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
| calibrated_uncertainty_in_contextual_questions | This criterion evaluates whether an answer appropriately expresses uncertainty when the question     |
|                                                | involves subjective, inferential, or contextually ambiguous visual interpretation — without over- or |
|                                                | under-qualifying. It is applicable ONLY when the question demands interpretation, inference, or      |
|                                                | contextual reasoning (e.g., ‘what is the mood?’, ‘how does this feel?’, ‘what might be happening?’), |
|                                                | and the visual evidence is ambiguous, insufficient, or requires non-literal inference. The answer    |
|                                                | must use calibrated uncertainty markers (e.g., ‘likely’, ‘appears tobe’, ‘based on visual cues’,    |
|                                                | ‘possibly’, ‘it seems like’) without overgeneralizing (e.g., ‘definitely’, ‘always’, ‘clearly’) or   |
|                                                | under-qualifying (e.g., ‘it’s this’, ‘it’s that’) — and must avoid definitive claims when evidence   |
|                                                | is ambiguous. It is a tiebreaker only when both answers are factually correct and visually grounded  |
|                                                | — and only for questions where uncertainty is contextually warranted. This criterion is inapplicable |
|                                                | to literal, measurable, or inventory-style questions — which are evaluated under other criteria —    |
|                                                | and must be explicitly excluded from evaluation for questions with clear, unambiguous visual         |
|                                                | evidence. Workers must prioritize factual accuracy — and this criterion must not override visual     |
|                                                | grounding or factual consistency. It does NOT penalize grounded inferences — only hallucinations or  |
|                                                | overconfidence in ambiguous contexts. The criterion must be applied conservatively — and must not be |
|                                                | used to penalize factually correct answers for being ‘less certain’ — only for inappropriate or      |
|                                                | excessive uncertainty markers. Workers must verify that the answer is factually correct and visually |
|                                                | grounded — and if either is violated, the answer is disqualified from this criterion — regardless of |
|                                                | uncertainty expression. The criterion is not a substitute for visualgrounding or factual            |
|                                                | consistency — and must be evaluated only after those are confirmed. It is not applicable to          |
|                                                | questions asking for literal, measurable, or compositional answers —and must defer to other         |
|                                                | criteria for such cases. The description must include a clear example: for a question ‘what is the   |
|                                                | mood?’, an answer ‘somber, likely due to the dim lighting’ is appropriate — while ‘dark and somber’  |
|                                                | without justification is overconfident and penalized — and ‘it’s hard to say’ without context is     |
|                                                | under-qualified. The criterion must explicitly state that it is onlyapplicable to questions where   |
|                                                | uncertainty is contextually warranted — and must not be used to penalize factually correct answers   |
|                                                | for being ‘too vague’. It must also clarify that ‘calibrated uncertainty’ means using appropriate,   |
|                                                | context-sensitive language — not just adding ‘maybe’ or ‘perhaps’ — and must avoid penalizing        |
|                                                | answers that are factually correct for being less specific. Finally,it must emphasize that this     |
|                                                | criterion is not a tiebreaker — and must not be applied to factuallyincorrect answers — even if     |
|                                                | they appear more uncertain. Workers must prioritize factual accuracy— and uncertainty is only a     |
|                                                | tool to express epistemic limitation — not a substitute for correctness.                             |
